                    - text: genetic models of PD, HD, AD, and frontotemporal dementia.
                    - link "122" [ref=e640] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r122"
                      - superscript [ref=e641]: "122"
                  - paragraph [ref=e642]:
                    - text: In primary mouse hippocampal neurons, R-GECO was targeted to the mitochondria using two targeting sequences from cytochrome C oxidase VIII added to the
                    - text: "-terminus. These experiments demonstrated that dendritic mitochondria had transient mitochondrial"
                    - text: elevations that were reduced when knocking out quality control proteins for mitochondrial protein import.
                    - link "138" [ref=e643] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r138"
                      - superscript [ref=e644]: "138"
                    - text: Mito-R-GECO has also been expressed in the rodent brain by intracortical injections of an adeno-associated expression virus.
                    - link "132" [ref=e645] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r132"
                      - superscript [ref=e646]: "132"
                    - superscript [ref=e647]: ","
                    - link "139" [ref=e648] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r139"
                      - superscript [ref=e649]: "139"
                    - text: Using this paradigm, mitochondrial
                    - text: uptake was correlated with higher firing rates of pyramidal neurons in slice preparations.
                    - link "140" [ref=e650] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r140"
                      - superscript [ref=e651]: "140"
                    - emphasis [ref=e652]: In vivo
                    - text: imaging of mito-R-GECO in zebrafish sensory neurons was used to determine
                    - text: dynamics during axon degeneration, where axotomy increased mitochondrial
                    - text: after cytoplasmic
                    - text: influx just prior to fragmentation.
                    - link "141" [ref=e653] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r141"
                      - superscript [ref=e654]: "141"
                - generic [ref=e655]:
                  - heading "2.7. FRET-Based GECIs" [level=3] [ref=e656]
                  - paragraph [ref=e657]:
                    - text: As neurodegenerative conditions progress over weeks, months, and years, intensity-based
                    - text: sensors are not optimal for chronic, comparative measurements of
                    - text: dynamics. Thus, ratiometric GECIs, often based on FRET, are more commonly used to study pathological
                    - text: dynamics. N33D1cpv measures
                    - text: on the outer surface of mitochondria and consists of a CFP and cpVenus FRET pair among the calmodulin
                    - text: "-binding domain that is localized to the mitochondrial outer membrane by an"
                    - text: "-terminal fusion of the mitochondrial protein Tom20."
                    - link "142" [ref=e658] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r142"
                      - superscript [ref=e659]: "142"
                    - text: When expressed in HeLa cells,
                    - text: hotspots from
                    - text: to 200 to
                    - text: were observed in response to histamine stimulation a limited area just outside of the mitochondria.
                    - link "143" [ref=e660] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r143"
                      - superscript [ref=e661]: "143"
                    - text: A newer version of the N33D1cpv biosensor, i.e., N33D3cp, with a higher affinity for
                    - text: was expressed in HeLa cells and showed that IP3R channels participate in reticular
                    - text: leak toward mitochondria.
                    - link "144" [ref=e662] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r144"
                      - superscript [ref=e663]: "144"
                  - paragraph [ref=e664]:
                    - text: Yellow cameleon 3.6 (YC3.6) is another FRET-based
                    - text: biosensor, which uses a CFP and YFP FRET pair flanking a calmodulin and M13
                    - text: "-binding motif. The YC3.6 biosensor, which was localized to mitochondria with a cytochrome oxidase subunit eight localization sequence, was used to evaluate neuronal function"
                    - emphasis [ref=e665]: in vivo
                    - text: in a mouse model of AD and identified that an increase in mito-
                    - text: was associated with neuronal death.
                    - link "133" [ref=e666] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r133"
                      - superscript [ref=e667]: "133"
                    - text: This study explicitly shows that mitochondrial
                    - text: "influx correlates with neurodegeneration, which is a common hypothesis in the field and assumed to occur in many models of neurodegenerative disease (see reviews: Verma et al.; Muller et al.; Ryan et al.; Calvo-Rodriguez et al.)."
                    - link "128" [ref=e668] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r128"
                      - superscript [ref=e669]: "128"
                    - superscript [ref=e670]: ","
                    - link "145" [ref=e671] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r145"
                      - superscript [ref=e672]: "145"
                    - emphasis
                    - emphasis [ref=e673]:
                      - superscript [ref=e674]: –
                    - link "147" [ref=e675] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r147"
                      - superscript [ref=e676]: "147"
                    - text: However, there are a few other studies that directly demonstrate that mitochondrial
                    - text: influx is specifically a driver of neuronal death after injury or in disease models. Therefore, mitochondrial
                    - text: biosensors applied to models of neurodegeneration, especially
                    - emphasis [ref=e677]: in vivo
                    - text: ", would be promising for future research given the availability of biosensors and the importance of definitively elucidating the role of mitochondrial"
                    - text: in neurodegeneration to develop therapeutic interventions.
              - generic [ref=e678]:
                - heading "3. Cell Signaling Pathways" [level=2] [ref=e679]: 3. Cell Signaling Pathways
                - paragraph [ref=e680]:
                  - text: Signaling pathways, involving receptors, ligands, transducers, messengers, and regulators, enable cells to adapt metabolism to changes in their environment.
                  - link "148" [ref=e681] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#r148"
                    - superscript [ref=e682]: "148"
                  - text: Although many signaling components, such as keystone kinases and transcription factors, have been identified, capturing real-time activity in neurons remains difficult. In the following section, we explore key signaling pathways implicated in neurodegenerative diseases. For each pathway, we discuss genetically encoded biosensors specifically designed to monitor their activity, emphasizing evidence from neurological contexts and their demonstrated efficiency and feasibility in both
                  - emphasis [ref=e683]: in vitro
                  - text: and
                  - emphasis [ref=e684]: in vivo
                  - text: settings.
                - generic [ref=e685]:
                  - heading "3.1. AMP-Activated Protein Kinase (AMPK)" [level=3] [ref=e686]
                  - paragraph [ref=e687]:
                    - text: The AMPK signaling pathway regulates cellular energetic and homeostatic mechanisms through direct and indirect processes.
                    - link "149" [ref=e688] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r149"
                      - superscript [ref=e689]: "149"
                    - emphasis
                    - emphasis [ref=e690]:
                      - superscript [ref=e691]: –
                    - link "151" [ref=e692] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r151"
                      - superscript [ref=e693]: "151"
                    - text: AMPK can be activated by AMP accumulation in conditions where ATP and ADP are depleted and can also be activated by the
                    - text: "-dependent/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase. Once triggered, AMPK initiates a cellular energy conservation program that inhibits energy-consuming lipid and protein biosynthetic processes while enhancing catabolic pathways such as glucose uptake, collectively aiming to restore cellular ATP levels."
                    - link "152" [ref=e694] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r152"
                      - superscript [ref=e695]: "152"
                    - text: AMPK has a complex and context-sensitive role in multiple neurodegenerative conditions, including AD, glaucoma, and ALS.
                    - link "153" [ref=e696] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r153"
                      - superscript [ref=e697]: "153"
                    - superscript [ref=e698]: ","
                    - link "154" [ref=e699] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r154"
                      - superscript [ref=e700]: "154"
                    - superscript [ref=e701]: ","
                    - link "156" [ref=e702] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r156"
                      - superscript [ref=e703]: "156"
                    - text: Activating AMPK can safeguard neurons from neurodegeneration by enhancing energy production through glycolysis and oxidative respiration. However, excessive or prolonged activation may cause harm
                    - emphasis [ref=e704]: via
                    - text: energy depletion and disrupted metabolic balance.
                    - link "155" [ref=e705] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r155"
                      - superscript [ref=e706]: "155"
                    - emphasis
                    - emphasis [ref=e707]:
                      - superscript [ref=e708]: –
                    - link "157" [ref=e709] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r157"
                      - superscript [ref=e710]: "157"
                  - generic [ref=e711]:
                    - heading "3.1.1. ABKAR" [level=4] [ref=e712]
                    - paragraph [ref=e713]:
                      - text: AMPK/BRSK activity reporter (ABKAR) is a second-generation FRET-based biosensor that monitors the activities of two critical kinases, AMPK and BR serine/threonine kinase (BRSK). ABKAR consists of an AMPK substrate peptide that interacts with a fork head–associated 1 domain (FHA1) between a Cerulean3 and cpVenusE172 FRET pair. The sensor demonstrates a nearly 40% dynamic range, which represents a twofold enhancement in dynamic range compared with the first-generation AMPKAR.
                      - link "158" [ref=e714] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r158"
                        - superscript [ref=e715]: "158"
                      - superscript [ref=e716]: ","
                      - link "159" [ref=e717] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r159"
                        - superscript [ref=e718]: "159"
                      - text: ABKAR was tested
                      - emphasis [ref=e719]: in vitro
                      - text: in Cos7, HeLa, and HEK293T cell lines, as well as in primary rat and mouse hippocampal neurons by applying ionomycin or 2-deoxyglucose (2-DG).
                      - link "160" [ref=e720] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r160"
                        - superscript [ref=e721]: "160"
                      - text: Addition of 2-DG led to a 10% to 20% increase in ABKAR activity in primary hippocampal neurons by 10 min after administration.
                      - link "160" [ref=e722] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r160"
                        - superscript [ref=e723]: "160"
                - generic [ref=e724]:
                  - heading "3.2. Mechanistic Target of Rapamycin (mTOR)" [level=3] [ref=e725]
                  - paragraph [ref=e726]:
                    - text: When nutrients abound, mTOR expedites protein and lipid synthesis, thereby supporting cell growth and proliferation while inhibiting degradative pathways.
                    - link "161" [ref=e727] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r161"
                      - superscript [ref=e728]: "161"
                    - emphasis
                    - emphasis [ref=e729]:
                      - superscript [ref=e730]: –
                    - link "163" [ref=e731] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r163"
                      - superscript [ref=e732]: "163"
                    - text: In numerous neurodegenerative disorders, mTOR also plays a role in activating autophagy to clear cytotoxic protein aggregates, such as tau and amyloid-
                    - text: in AD. Inhibiting mTOR benefits AD, PD, and HD by reducing levels of
                    - text: ","
                    - text: "-synuclein, and mutant huntingtin."
                    - link "164" [ref=e733] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r164"
                      - superscript [ref=e734]: "164"
                    - emphasis
                    - emphasis [ref=e735]:
                      - superscript [ref=e736]: –
                    - link "166" [ref=e737] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r166"
                      - superscript [ref=e738]: "166"
                  - generic [ref=e739]:
                    - heading "3.2.1. AIMTOR" [level=4] [ref=e740]
                    - paragraph [ref=e741]:
                      - text: Activity indicator of mTOR (AIMTOR) uses bioluminescent resonance energy transfer (BRET) to monitor mTOR activity. The biosensor includes a mTORC1 substrate peptide from Unc-51, such as autophagy activating kinase 1, and a WW ligand–binding domain positioned between a nano-luciferase and the yellow YPET fluorescent protein.
                      - link "167" [ref=e742] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r167"
                        - superscript [ref=e743]: "167"
                      - text: Upon phosphorylation by mTOR, the binding of the WW domain to the phosphorylated residue induces a conformational shift, bringing the donor and acceptor into closer proximity and then enhancing the BRET signal. AIMTOR demonstrates a BRET dynamic range of
                      - text: units between nutrient-rich and fasting conditions.
                      - link "167" [ref=e744] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r167"
                        - superscript [ref=e745]: "167"
                      - text: Versions of the biosensor targeted to the cytosol [retaining the nuclear export signal (NES)], nucleus [by removing the NES via XbaI/SalI digestion and ligating with complementary oligonucleotides encoding a
                      - text: "-terminus SV40 nuclear localization signal, or nuclear localization sequence (NLS)], mitochondria (by removing the NES via XbaI/SalI digestion and ligating with complementary oligonucleotides encoding a mitochondrial targeting sequence from the last 29 amino acids of the"
                      - text: "-terminus of human monoamine oxidase), and lysosomal surfaces (by removing the NES via XbaI/SalI digestion and ligating with complementary oligonucleotides encoding a 15-amino-acid"
                      - text: "-terminus CAAX lysosomal targeting sequence from the Rheb protein) were developed, enabling investigations into compartment-specific mTOR signaling. Depolarization in primary cultured hippocampal neurons increased mean BRET intensity, indicating elevated mTORC1 activity in response to circuit activity. In primary neuronal cultures from Fmr1 knockout and Shank3Δ11 mice (models of autism spectrum disorder), AIMTOR showed higher basal mTORC1 signaling than in wild-type neurons. However, unlike wild-type neurons, depolarization did not further increase AIMTOR BRET signals in these mutants, revealing mTORC1 dysfunction in autism spectrum disorders."
                      - link "167" [ref=e746] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r167"
                        - superscript [ref=e747]: "167"
                  - generic [ref=e748]:
                    - heading "3.2.2. TORCAR" [level=4] [ref=e749]
                    - paragraph [ref=e750]:
                      - text: mTORC1 activity reporter (TORCAR) is a FRET-based biosensor consisting of full-length 4EBP1, an mTORC1 target protein, linked between a cerulean and YPet FRET pair on its
                      - text: "- and"
                      - text: "-terminals, respectively."
                      - link "168" [ref=e751] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r168"
                        - superscript [ref=e752]: "168"
                      - text: Although multiple configurations of FRET pairs were tested during development of TORCAR, the dynamic range appears limited in that NIH-3T3 cells showed only a roughly 15% increase in FRET ratio in response to PDGF stimulation.
                      - link "168" [ref=e753] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r168"
                        - superscript [ref=e754]: "168"
                      - text: TORCAR has yet to be examined
                      - emphasis [ref=e755]: in vivo
                      - text: .
                - generic [ref=e756]:
                  - heading "3.3. Phosphatase and Tensin Homolog (PTEN)" [level=3] [ref=e757]
                  - paragraph [ref=e758]:
                    - text: PTEN counteracts the PI3K/Akt/mTOR axis to regulate metabolic activity, ensuring balance in biosynthetic processes.
                    - link "169" [ref=e759] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r169"
                      - superscript [ref=e760]: "169"
                    - text: When PTEN is reduced or inactivated, Akt signaling frequently becomes hyperactive, leading to excessive biosynthetic activity and disproportionately elevated energy demands.
                    - link "169" [ref=e761] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r169"
                      - superscript [ref=e762]: "169"
                    - text: Enzymatically inactive PTEN in AD contributes to abnormal tau phosphorylation and
                    - text: accumulation, intensifying disease progression.
                    - link "170" [ref=e763] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r170"
                      - superscript [ref=e764]: "170"
                    - superscript [ref=e765]: ","
                    - link "171" [ref=e766] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r171"
                      - superscript [ref=e767]: "171"
                    - text: Meanwhile, PD models indicate that PTEN-mediated inhibition of PI3K/Akt signaling detrimentally influences dopaminergic neuron survival, positioning PTEN as a potential target for neuroprotection.
                    - link "172" [ref=e768] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r172"
                      - superscript [ref=e769]: "172"
                    - superscript [ref=e770]: ","
                    - link "173" [ref=e771] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r173"
                      - superscript [ref=e772]: "173"
                    - text: Indeed, in stroke, brain injury, spinal cord injury, and optic nerve crush, suppressing PTEN boosts neuronal survival and fosters axon regeneration, underscoring its therapeutic promise for CNS repair.
                    - link "174" [ref=e773] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r174"
                      - superscript [ref=e774]: "174"
                    - emphasis
                    - emphasis [ref=e775]:
                      - superscript [ref=e776]: –
                    - link "176" [ref=e777] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r176"
                      - superscript [ref=e778]: "176"
                  - generic [ref=e779]:
                    - heading "3.3.1. G- and R-PTEN" [level=4] [ref=e780]
                    - paragraph [ref=e781]:
                      - text: G-PTEN is a FLIM biosensor that consists of PTEN flanked by an
                      - text: "-terminal fusion of eGFP and a"
                      - text: "-terminal fusion of the dead-YFP variant sREACh. G-PTEN was optimized to reduce the effects of PTEN overexpression and demonstrates a roughly 10% dynamic range. The red-shifted R-PTEN is similar but consists of"
                      - text: "-terminal mCyRFP2 and"
                      - text: "-terminal mMaroon1 for multiplexed imaging with other shorter wavelength biosensors. G- and R-PTEN have been used to track PTEN activity in"
                      - emphasis [ref=e782]: C. elegans
                      - text: and mouse neurons
                      - emphasis [ref=e783]: in vivo
                      - text: . High-resolution
                      - emphasis [ref=e784]: in vivo
                      - text: imaging of G-PTEN expressed in mouse pyramidal neurons demonstrated increased PTEN activity in response to CRISPR-mediated knockout of insulin-like growth factor 1 receptor and tuberous sclerosis complex 2.
                      - link "177" [ref=e785] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r177"
                        - superscript [ref=e786]: "177"
                      - text: The role of PTEN signaling in neuronal circuit rewiring was directly demonstrated by
                      - emphasis [ref=e787]: in vivo
                      - text: imaging of G-PTEN in the whisker barrel cortex during a whisker deprivation model. Interestingly, although whisker deprivation led to a roughly 8% increase in G-PTEN FLIM in inhibitory neurons, PTEN activation was actually slightly reduced in excitatory neurons with presumably less circuit activity.
                      - link "177" [ref=e788] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r177"
                        - superscript [ref=e789]: "177"
                - generic [ref=e790]:
                  - heading "3.4. Hypoxia Inducible Factor-1α (HIF-1α)" [level=3] [ref=e791]:
                    - text: 3.4. Hypoxia Inducible Factor-1
                    - emphasis [ref=e792]: α
                    - text: (HIF-1
                    - emphasis [ref=e793]: α
                    - text: )
                  - paragraph [ref=e794]:
                    - text: HIF-
                    - text: signaling senses depletion of oxygen levels and controls mitochondrial function and cellular bioenergetics to maintain cellular function in an anaerobic environment.
                    - link "178" [ref=e795] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r178"
                      - superscript [ref=e796]: "178"
                    - superscript [ref=e797]: ","
                    - link "179" [ref=e798] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r179"
                      - superscript [ref=e799]: "179"
                    - text: Under physiological conditions where oxygen is present, HIF-
                    - text: protein is constantly degraded
                    - emphasis [ref=e800]: via
                    - text: its oxygen-dependent degradation (ODD) domain, being tagged for proteasomal degradation by prolyl-4-hydroxylases (PHD). When oxygen levels are depleted, PHD activity is substrate-limited, allowing HIF-
                    - text: protein to build up and upregulate expression of numerous target genes, including glycolytic enzymes, glucose transporters, fatty acid synthesis enzymes, and components required for autophagy.
                    - link "180" [ref=e801] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r180"
                      - superscript [ref=e802]: "180"
                    - text: In AD, HIF-
                    - text: counters neuronal loss by a mechanism that upregulates glycolysis and beta-site amyloid precursor protein cleaving enzyme 1 expression, potentially fueling amyloidogenic processing.
                    - link "181" [ref=e803] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r181"
                      - superscript [ref=e804]: "181"
                    - superscript [ref=e805]: ","
                    - link "182" [ref=e806] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r182"
                      - superscript [ref=e807]: "182"
                    - text: Ischemic stroke studies highlight a time-dependent role for HIF-
                    - text: ", which initially exacerbates cell death but later confers pro-survival benefits."
                    - link "183" [ref=e808] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r183"
                      - superscript [ref=e809]: "183"
                    - text: TBI models reveal that HIF-
                    - text: upregulation promotes neuroprotection, partly
                    - emphasis [ref=e810]: via
                    - text: erythropoietin induction.
                    - link "184" [ref=e811] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r184"
                      - superscript [ref=e812]: "184"
                    - superscript [ref=e813]: ","
                    - link "185" [ref=e814] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r185"
                      - superscript [ref=e815]: "185"
                  - generic [ref=e816]:
                    - heading "3.4.1. ODD-Luc and ODD-GFP" [level=4] [ref=e817]
                    - paragraph [ref=e818]:
                      - text: The ODD type of HIF-
                      - text: biosensors fuse the ODD domain (amino acids 530-603) from HIF-
                      - text: onto a fluorescent or bioluminescent reporter (ODD-GFP and ODD-Luc, respectively). Like endogenous HIF-
                      - text: ", ODD-GFP increases in protein abundance and thus fluorescence intensity when oxygen levels are depleted."
                      - link "186" [ref=e819] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r186"
                        - superscript [ref=e820]: "186"
                      - text: ODD-GFP has been expressed in
                      - emphasis [ref=e821]: Drosophila
                      - text: larvae and was able to detect differences in cellular oxygenation states dependent on distance from respiratory tubes.
                      - link "186" [ref=e822] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r186"
                        - superscript [ref=e823]: "186"
                      - text: The ODD-Luc reporter, a bioluminescent fusion of the HIF-
                      - text: ODD domain and firefly luciferase, monitors HIF-
                      - text: stabilization. In neuroblastoma cells, it shows dose-dependent stabilization within 2 h by PHD inhibitors such as ciclopirox (CPO, targets iron-binding site), 3,4-dihydroxybenzoate, and dimethyloxaloylglycine.
                      - link "187" [ref=e824] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r187"
                        - superscript [ref=e825]: "187"
                      - text: In hypoxia, ODD-Luc activity increases sixfold after 2 h, but only twofold in glucose-free media, indicating glucose dependency. This glucose-dependent HIF-
                      - text: stabilization in hypoxia primarily depends on ATP levels, not PHD activity or transcriptional changes.
                      - link "188" [ref=e826] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r188"
                        - superscript [ref=e827]: "188"
                    - paragraph [ref=e828]:
                      - text: Signals from HIF-
                      - text: biosensors can be further amplified by combining the ODD protein degradation motif with a gene regulatory element that is recognized by HIF-
                      - text: under hypoxic conditions. The hypoxia response element (HRE) is recognized by HIF transcriptional complexes when HIF-
                      - text: can be accumulated during hypoxia. By expressing ODD-Luc under the transcriptional control of HRE, HRE-ODD-Luc showed a fourfold increase in expression after 16 h of hypoxia.
                      - link "189" [ref=e829] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r189"
                        - superscript [ref=e830]: "189"
                - generic [ref=e831]:
                  - heading "3.5. Nuclear Factor Erythroid 2-Related Factor 2 (Nrf2)" [level=3] [ref=e832]
                  - paragraph [ref=e833]:
                    - text: Nrf2 governs cellular redox balance by detecting oxidative or electrophilic stressors. Once activated, Nrf2 relocates to the nucleus, where it stimulates transcriptional programs that enhance antioxidant capacity and protective detoxification, thereby maintaining mitochondrial function and safeguarding ATP production against oxidative damage.
                    - link "190" [ref=e834] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r190"
                      - superscript [ref=e835]: "190"
                    - superscript [ref=e836]: ","
                    - link "191" [ref=e837] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r191"
                      - superscript [ref=e838]: "191"
                    - text: Nrf2 also regulates glucose uptake and increases both NADH and NADPH cytoplasmic levels in neurons.
                    - link "192" [ref=e839] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r192"
                      - superscript [ref=e840]: "192"
                    - text: The Nrf2 pathway has been linked to mechanisms in neurological disorders. In AD and PD, diminished Nrf2 activity correlates with heightened neuroinflammation, and Nrf2 activation reduces inflammatory damage by enhancing autophagy.
                    - link "193" [ref=e841] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r193"
                      - superscript [ref=e842]: "193"
                    - superscript [ref=e843]: ","
                    - link "194" [ref=e844] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r194"
                      - superscript [ref=e845]: "194"
                    - text: In HD, upregulating Nrf2 clears mutant huntingtin aggregates and bolsters mitochondrial function.
                    - link "195" [ref=e846] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r195"
                      - superscript [ref=e847]: "195"
                    - superscript [ref=e848]: ","
                    - link "196" [ref=e849] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r196"
                      - superscript [ref=e850]: "196"
                    - text: In ALS, Nrf2 upregulation preserves neuromuscular junctions and prolongs survival.
                    - link "197" [ref=e851] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r197"
                      - superscript [ref=e852]: "197"
                    - superscript [ref=e853]: ","
                    - link "198" [ref=e854] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r198"
                      - superscript [ref=e855]: "198"
                    - text: Nrf2 overexpression in astrocytes enhances neuroprotection, whereas its neural activation addresses oxidative stress in multiple sclerosis, ischemic stroke, and retinal ganglion cell degeneration.
                    - link "199" [ref=e856] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r199"
                      - superscript [ref=e857]: "199"
                    - emphasis
                    - emphasis [ref=e858]:
                      - superscript [ref=e859]: –
                    - link "202" [ref=e860] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r202"
                      - superscript [ref=e861]: "202"
                  - generic [ref=e862]:
                    - heading "3.5.1. POINTER" [level=4] [ref=e863]
                    - paragraph [ref=e864]:
                      - text: Pericellular oxygen–insensitive Nrf2 total level reporter (POINTER) is a biosensor that uses transcriptional regulation of miniGFP to measure Nrf2 activity. The transcriptional regulatory promoter uses four repeats of antioxidant response element (ARE) sequences from the promoter of NAD(P)H quinone dehydrogenase 1 (NQO1) to drive the expression of miniGFP. POINTER was evaluated in cultured human cerebral microvascular endothelial cells, where it demonstrated clear distinctions in Nrf2 activity between normoxia and physiological hypoxia. The fluorescence intensity of miniGFP correlated strongly with real-time PCR measurements of oxidative stress–related transcripts, including NQO1, heme oxygenase 1, and glutamate–cysteine ligase catalytic subunit, confirming its functional accuracy.
                      - link "203" [ref=e865] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#r203"
                        - superscript [ref=e866]: "203"
                - generic [ref=e867]:
                  - heading "3.6. Guidelines for Using These Biosensors in Studies of Neurodegenerative Diseases" [level=3] [ref=e868]
                  - paragraph [ref=e869]:
                    - text: The advent of genetically encoded biosensors has dramatically transformed our capability to observe substrate metabolism in neurons with remarkable spatial and temporal precision. When employing these biosensors to monitor substrate metabolism in living animals, several important factors must be considered. First, specificity and responsiveness are crucial. The physiological levels of different metabolic substrates in mammalian cells vary widely. For metabolites present in high abundance (such as glucose, ATP, lactate, and NAD+), which typically range from 0.1 to 5 mM,
                    - link "102" [ref=e870] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r102"
                      - superscript [ref=e871]: "102"
                    - superscript [ref=e872]: ","
                    - link "204" [ref=e873] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r204"
                      - superscript [ref=e874]: "204"
                    - superscript [ref=e875]: ","
                    - link "205" [ref=e876] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r205"
                      - superscript [ref=e877]: "205"
                    - text: biosensors with lower affinity are recommended. For metabolites found in lower concentrations (such as NADH, NADP+, and NADPH), typically ranging from 0.1 to
                    - text: ","
                    - link "103" [ref=e878] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r103"
                      - superscript [ref=e879]: "103"
                    - superscript [ref=e880]: ","
                    - link "206" [ref=e881] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r206"
                      - superscript [ref=e882]: "206"
                    - superscript [ref=e883]: ","
                    - link "207" [ref=e884] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#r207"
                      - superscript [ref=e885]: "207"
                    - text: high-affinity biosensors are preferable. The affinity of each biosensor is listed in
                    - link "Table 1" [ref=e886] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#t001"
                    - text: ; however, some sensors may have limited practical utility because their binding affinities fall outside the physiological range.
                  - generic [ref=e887]:
                    - heading "Table 1." [level=4] [ref=e888]
                    - paragraph [ref=e890]: Genetically encoded fluorescent biosensors for the study of neurodegeneration.

                      - /url: table/t001/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1181]:
                    - text: In addition, the effect of pH on sensor performance is a critical consideration. Many cpFP-based biosensors are sensitive to pH fluctuations, which can affect their fluorescence readouts. Furthermore, significant glycolytic rate changes can alter intracellular pH, potentially interfering with sensor accuracy. To ensure accurate measurements, it may be necessary to correct for pH effects using a pH control sensor. Last, when combining different biosensors within the same cell
                    - emphasis [ref=e1182]: in vivo
                    - text: ", the fluorescence emission spectra of each sensor must be considered to avoid overlap and ensure a clear, distinct readout."
                  - paragraph [ref=e1183]:
                    - text: Overall, genetically encoded fluorescent sensors excel in providing detailed
                    - emphasis [ref=e1184]: in situ
                    - text: measurements, and integrating these tools with metabolomics and metabolic flux analysis can enhance our understanding of global metabolic profiles and networks. The ability to resolve metabolic dynamics within targeted cellular populations and at subcellular resolution makes biosensors extremely powerful tools for the investigation of neurodegeneration. However, as a field, we have been relatively slow to apply these tools, which has perhaps limited the development of improved next-generation sensors in the vein of the extremely optimized GECI family. We believe that stronger collaborative efforts between the neuroscience and biosensor design fields are needed to accelerate progress in this research area.
